| **[The World Model Planning Accelerator (WMPA): Hardware-Algorithm Co-Design for Embedded AI in Robotics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10316v1)** | 2026-11-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Embedded AI is moving from perception to prediction. Robots now use world models$\unicode{x2014}$learned simulators that predict the outcome of candidate actions in a compressed latent space before any is executed. Every predicted step must be computed on board within a tight power and thermal budget, yet the energy these steps fundamentally require, and how far real hardware sits from it, have not been characterized. We derive lower bounds on the arithmetic operations and the CMOS dynamic energy of a latent state transition from information theory and Boolean circuit complexity. Profiling a DreamerV3-style Recurrent State Space Model (RSSM) shows that every rollout operation is memory-bandwidth bound and that a GPU consumes at least $5.9\times10^{10}$ times the CMOS energy floor. Transient 3D thermal simulations show that this inefficiency is also a thermal one, since sustained back-to-back planning heats a millimeter-scale silicon tile to 556 K even behind an ideal copper heat spreader. Guided by this chain from bound to bottleneck, we propose the World Model Planning Accelerator (WMPA), a chip architecture for embedded world model planning that combines near-memory computation, real-time entropy estimation, variable-precision rollout units, and copy-on-write latent scratchpads. | **[Mobility Information Capacity of Multi-Robot Systems: Safety-Aware Maneuver Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10298v1)** | 2026-11-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multiple robots operating in shared workspaces must adjust their speed, direction, and trajectories to coordinate with one another and respond to changing tasks and disturbances. The available maneuver freedom depends on the dynamics, uncertainty, and safety margins, even for a fixed fleet size and workspace. However, robot count, workspace occupancy, and task throughput do not quantify how much the fleet can vary its motion or how reliably these variations can be observed. We develop a mobility information capacity (MIC) framework that jointly characterizes these two aspects. Mobility information is defined as the mutual information between a joint maneuver sequence and its noisy trajectory observation, and MIC maximizes this quantity under maneuver-resource constraints. For a linear Gaussian maneuver-to-observation model with feedback dynamics, we characterize the finite-horizon information benchmark and derive its stationary long-horizon spectral limit, accounting for heterogeneous mobility gains, colored uncertainty, and temporally coupled maneuver costs. We further map workspace-boundary, inter-robot separation, and actuator chance constraints into coupled maneuver-covariance restrictions and derive conditions under which covariance optimization reduces to resource allocation across mobility modes. Finite joint maneuver codebooks are then constructed through individual risk screening and noise-whitened trajectory separation, with bounds linking sequence recovery error and achieved information to the capacity benchmarks. An aerial-robot simulation study evaluates spectral convergence, safety-aware allocation, finite-codebook identification, and fleet-size scaling. The resulting framework complements occupancy and task-throughput measures by quantifying the maximum observable maneuver freedom supported by a mobility system.</p></details> | <details><summary>12-pa...</summary><p>12-page main article and 4-page supplementary material</p></details> |
| **[Who Taught the Robot That? Removing One User's Corrections from a Robot Policy That Learns Online](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10280v1)** | 2026-11-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A robot that keeps learning after deployment is corrected by several people in turn, and one of them may later ask to be removed. We show that whether the robot can forget that person depends on how people correct it. The goal of removal is the robot that would exist if the person had never been there, which we compute exactly in two simulated tasks with scripted users. When users correct the robot by taking over control, an ordered log of training updates is enough: retraining on the remaining sessions in their original order gives a robot as close to the goal as training noise allows. Deleting the person's data and retraining in one pass does not, even when the other users' corrections do not depend on the removed person, because it loses the order of training. When users only nudge the robot's action, the other users' corrections carry the removed person's influence, and no method we tested recovers the goal robot. Continuing to train without the person removes most of their habits, but not what their data in the training buffer keep alive. The log can identify one clearly harmful user, but not rank users by influence better than counting their corrections.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at the HumanRLI Workshop at CoRL 2026</p></details> |
| **[Agibot: An Open-Source Modular Swerve-Drive Robot Platform for Agricultural Robotics Research and Education](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10235v1)** | 2026-11-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agricultural production faces persistent labor shortages and increasing demand for automation, yet progress in agricultural robotics remains constrained by the limited availability of affordable, flexible, and accessible research platforms. The platform was evaluated through representative row-crop navigation and manipulation tasks under varying lighting and soil conditions. Experiments demonstrated reliable lane following, autonomous row-to-row navigation, target identification, seed dispensing, plant manipulation, and closed-loop verification of task success. These proof-of-concept tasks, controller-tuning studies, and integrated trials demonstrate the platform's research capabilities, while its accessible and reconfigurable design supports its use for hands-on robotics education. By combining affordability, modularity, open-source accessibility, configurable manipulation, and end-to-end sense-plan-act-verify autonomy, Agibot provides a practical platform for agricultural robotics research, technology development, and workforce training.</p></details> | <details><summary>14 pa...</summary><p>14 pages. Submitted to IEEE Transactions on AgriFood Electronics (TAFE)</p></details> |
| **[SyncTwin: Fast Digital Twin Construction and Synchronization for Safe Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.09920v3)** | 2026-11-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Accurate and safe robotic manipulation under dynamic and visually occluded conditions remains a core challenge in real-world deployment. During execution, SyncTwin continuously synchronizes the digital twin by tracking real-world object states and uses stored assets to complete partially observed geometry. The synchronized twin enables motion planners to compute collision-aware robot trajectories in simulation, with execution on the real robot closing the real-to-sim-to-real loop. Experiments in dynamic and occluded scenes show that SyncTwin improves manipulation performance and motion safety, demonstrating the effectiveness of digital twin synchronization for real-world robotic execution.</p></details> | <details><summary>IEEE ...</summary><p>IEEE Robotics and Automation Letters; Project Website: https://sync-twin.github.io/</p></details> |
| **[Locating Contact on Tendon-Driven Continuum Robots From Tendon Measurements Alone via Constrained Moving Horizon Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09877v1)** | 2026-11-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Tendon-driven continuum robots can reach through constrained spaces, but contact along their bodies is invisible to the sensors at their motors. When the obstacle geometry is known, however, non-penetration and complementarity make the contact force locally identifiable, and we prove that the resulting system with unknown contact inputs is locally exponentially strongly detectable. We present a constrained moving horizon estimator (MHE) that encodes these conditions and runs in real time as one quadratic program per sample, and we give a local, mode-wise robust stability result for it. In a finite- element benchmark of a two-section robot pressing and sliding on a wall, the estimator located contact to 0.9 mm and estimated contact force to 0.22 N, compared with 65 mm and 0.72 N for a tuned extended Kalman filter (EKF) with state projection. Under randomized model mismatch over 40 trials, including unmodeled tendon friction, it remained better in force and location in every trial. Perturbed demonstrations supply the missing negatives without encoding any rule, and evolutionary search fits the constraint, which captures driving rules or social-navigation preferences within a modelled taxonomy of norms. We use the mined constraint to shield driving and navigation policies on Bench2Drive and HA-VLN-CE. Second, an evolutionary PDDL planner: an LLM proposes and repairs plans, programmatic validators decide which ones survive, and the validated portion of the plan grows over iterations. On open-world ALFWorld and NL-PDDL Blocksworld, it approaches classical planning when the goal vocabulary agrees with the action model, and it stays robust when the two disagree and classical planning fails. Third, a constrained execution loop: Nav2 uses the costmap to ground the plan into waypoints, which are checked against the mined constraint. Violations and execution failures update the PDDL problem for re-planning. We demonstrate the full pipeline in Gazebo and on a Clearpath Jackal robot. More details and videos are on the project page: https://nbhavyasai.github.io/evoplan/</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at CoRL 2026. Project page: https://nbhavyasai.github.io/evoplan/</p></details> |
| **[VisionSIM: Beyond Idealized Sensors - A Multi-Modal Simulator for Robot Perception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08368v1)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Robotic perception increasingly relies on heterogeneous sensor suites combining cameras with emerging modalities (event, SPAD, iToF/dToF, and thermal). Yet existing simulators idealize these sensors, hiding critical modality-specific failure modes that limit real-world deployment. We present VisionSIM, a unified, physically grounded simulation platform that elevates sensor realism to a first-class concern by modeling the raw physical measurement process for diverse conventional and emerging modalities. We expose fine-grained control over physical sensor parameters, reproducing failure modes overlooked by general-purpose simulators: pileup and glare for LiDAR; phase wrapping, saturation, and motion artifacts for iToF; refractory drops for event cameras; and history-dependent emission for thermal cameras. This enables principled cross-modality comparison on identical scenes and trajectories. We release the platform and a large indoor dataset with synchronized raw measurements and rich ground-truth annotations.</p></details> |  |
| **[Transformer-Based Trajectory Prescription for sEMG-Optimizing Robot-Mediated Rehabilitation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08295v1)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Robot-mediated rehabilitation has the potential to deliver high-intensity, individualized therapy, but existing systems (which have not consistently improved outcomes) typically prescribe kinematic trajectories rather than explicitly targeting the neuromotor activations thought to support recovery. To address this gap, and ultimately enable activation-optimizing rehabilitation robot behavior, we present a personalizable transformer-based model mapping individuals' muscle activations (as measured via surface electromyography) to prescribed contact force trajectories when interacting with an end-effector rehabilitation robot. The model uses a transformer encoder-decoder leveraging rotary positional embeddings (RoPE) to generate a latent representation of trajectory-relevant and user-specific neuromotor factors, and augments trajectory prediction with flow matching to produce a distribution over plausible prescriptions biased toward the training distribution. Using data from 16 healthy participants completing 15 isometric trajectory tracking tasks, we evaluate generalization across held-out tasks and users, as well as the feasibility of user-specific fine-tuning within a therapy-session timescale. The model achieves mean trajectory prediction errors of <8% on held out tasks and users, with effectively instantaneous deterministic inference and sub-second sampling of trajectory distributions. An initial test using the model during live rehabilitation tasks demonstrates that, on average, participants reach the target neuromotor activations with just 15% error accumulated throughout the task. These results provide a proof of concept for data-driven, activation-targeted trajectory prescription in robot-mediated rehabilitation and establish a foundation for future validation in clinical populations. Data and code have been open-sourced as part of the OpenRobotRehab project on SimTK.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted for presentation and publication in the 2026 Conference on Robot Learning (CoRL). PMLR, 2026</p></details> |
| **[GUARD: Sensitivity-aware Uncertainty Attribution for Safe Traditional and Learning-based Robot Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.23312v2)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This paper presents the framework GUARD (Guided robot control via Uncertainty attribution and probAbilistic kernel optimization for Risk-aware Decision making) that combines a technique from probabilistic state estimation with an uncertainty identification method to derive critical thresholds for robot control algorithms. The first part focuses on complementing a method for probabilistic kernel optimization (PKO) by a sensitivity-aware uncertainty attribution technique. Next, the estimated uncertainties and their sources are forwarded to the robot controllers to adapt safety margins and trigger the initialization of safe backup trajectories. To validate GUARD's applicability and performance, experiments are conducted on a traditional model predictive control (MPC) algorithm and a reinforcement learning (RL) based controller. The results demonstrate that GUARD reduces collision rates by 44-67% in the RL-based evaluation, while decreasing backup policy activation by 50-75% in the medium- and high-uncertainty profiles. In the MPC simulation, GUARD successfully avoids collisions. By unifying the probabilistic kernel optimization algorithm from robot state estimation with the uncertainty estimation method for learning-based control, GUARD brings together traditional techniques and learning for safe and efficient robot applications.</p></details> | <details><summary>9 pag...</summary><p>9 pages, 9 figures, 2 tables. Substantially revised and expanded version with updated title and authors. Extended validation using model predictive control and reinforcement learning-based control</p></details> |
| **[Open-HumanWAM: An Open Recipe for Scaling Human and Robot Experience for Embodied Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06817v2)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Egocentric human video offers a scalable source of manipulation priors for robot learning, yet translating raw data scale into generalizable robot policies demands rigorous data curation and training recipes. We introduce Open-HumanWAM, the first fully open recipe for scaling egocentric pretraining to 50,000 hours, anchored by Open-HumanWAM-50K---a curated egocentric video corpus densely annotated with hand motion and natural language. Leveraging this corpus, we systematically investigate pure human pretraining via closed-loop simulation evaluations and uncover an empirical log-linear scaling law linking pretraining data hours to downstream robot success rates. Grounded in these findings, we develop HumanWAM-Vanilla, a world--action model pretrained exclusively on 50k hours of egocentric human video. Upon robot post-training, HumanWAM-Vanilla reaches 92.08% success on RoboTwin 2.0 Clean2Clean and 68.88% on Clean2Random, alongside a 14.17% mean success rate and a 19.00 mean progress score on RoboDojo. To push the capability frontier further, we explore joint human--robot pretraining with HumanWAM-Goal, a visual-goal-conditioned world--action model. Hybrid data scaling unlocks broad zero-shot generalization across both simulation and physical benchmarks, propelling post-trained RoboTwin 2.0 performance to new state-of-the-art levels: 92.70% on Clean2Clean and a substantial leap to 84.60% on Clean2Random. On physical robots, HumanWAM-Goal further exhibits emergent embodied in-context learning, dynamically adapting its manipulation behavior to unseen visual goals without parameter updates. To support reproducible scaling research in embodied AI, we open-source the Open-HumanWAM-50K corpus, data curation and pretraining pipelines, alongside the pretrained and post-trained HumanWAM checkpoints, inference code, and simulation evaluation suites.</p></details> | <details><summary>GitHu...</summary><p>GitHub: https://github.com/DAGroup-PKU/HumanNet/tree/Open-HumanWAM Project website: https://dagroup-pku.github.io/open-humanwam/</p></details> |
| **[RoboARCade: Benchmarking Agentic Robot Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08209v1)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Improvements in multi-modal capabilities have enabled agents to act directly as robot policies, a setting we refer to as agentic robot control (ARC). We introduce RoboARCade, a benchmark of long-horizon manipulation tasks across three embodiments designed to test in-context adaptation, memory, and reasoning for precise control. The tasks build on existing open-source benchmarks, which we make harder by widening the range of initial conditions. RoboARCade is easy to use and extend: all tasks run on a single simulation backend, MuJoCo, each task is a self-contained data pack, and agents connect through interchangeable interfaces, so new tasks, agents, and interfaces can be added independently. The benchmark separates leading agents: GPT-6 Astra achieves 49.8% compared with 43.6% for Claude Fable 5.1, leaving substantial room for improvement. Comparing two interfaces, we find that a richer visual interface adds only 1.5 to 6.8 points at 1.7 to 2.3 times the cost. Failures stem mostly from choosing a poor manipulation strategy or executing a good one imprecisely, rarely from misunderstanding the scene, and agents incorrectly claim success on 24% to 39% of attempted subtasks. Agents can also improve without human input: one successful demonstration in context raises Astra's score from 53.5% to 73.0% on a five-task subset, and a prompt the agent writes and revises itself reaches 72.0%. Code, videos and full traces are available at roboarcade.org.</p></details> |  |
| **[La-JEPA: Compact Robot Policies with Latent World Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08156v1)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>World-action models adapt pretrained video generators, and vision-language-action models build on pretrained vision-language backbones; both tie policy architecture and inference cost to large transformers designed for generation rather than control. It remains unclear how much these generation-oriented choices affect control. In this work, we introduce Latent Action JEPA (La-JEPA), a compact world-action model whose policy transformer is trained from scratch in the feature space of a pretrained V-JEPA 2.1 encoder. Following Fast-WAM, La-JEPA learns to predict future features jointly with actions during training and generates only actions at inference. Among policies without embodied pretraining, La-JEPA achieves state-of-the-art performance in clean and randomized RoboTwin 2.0 scenes and on LIBERO-Plus with correct instructions, while using far fewer parameters than the strongest competing policies. It runs substantially faster than Fast-WAM at comparable performance and outperforms the closest JEPA-based methods, LeapBot-WA and JEPA-WAM, on LIBERO-Plus and RoboTwin 2.0 without their pretrained policy backbones. On four real-robot tasks, its average success is comparable to pi0.5 and above non-embodied-pretrained baselines. Through a design study of the choices behind La-JEPA, we show that JEPA features outperform video VAE latents, most clearly out of distribution, and that training-time future prediction choices significantly impact the policy even when future prediction is skipped at inference. We correct the LIBERO-Plus instructions, as the officially released evaluator corrupts them for most variants, and re-evaluate several strong open baselines. Without a generative pretrained policy backbone, La-JEPA is better and faster than far larger policies, suggesting that robot foundation models may benefit from architectures designed for control rather than inherited from generation.</p></details> | <details><summary>30 pa...</summary><p>30 pages, 6 figures. Code: https://github.com/mt-clemente/lajepa</p></details> |
| **[Educational robotics beyond ROS: a custom library for direct transfer of Webots controllers in the Turtlebot3](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08142v1)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Robot Operating System (ROS) is becoming a standard in robotics courses at higher education, but its installation, configuration and distributed programming model can introduce a significant entry barrier in the new landscape of AI-based master and degree studies, where many students do not have a strong informatics background. This paper presents an educational experience that enables students to program a TurtleBot3 Burger directly in Python, while preserving a direct transfer path from simulation to the physical robot. The approach is based on a custom library that mirrors the Webots API, allowing the same controller code to be executed in the simulator and on the real TurtleBot3 with minimal modification. The library provides access to the robot motors, wheel encoders, inertial sensors and LiDAR, and is complemented by scripts that automate deployment and execution on the physical platform. It was used in a master-level course on introductory autonomous vehicles, and initial student feedback suggests that the approach reduces the perceived complexity of ROS-related infrastructure and helps learners focus on algorithmic logic and real-world behaviour.</p></details> |  |
| **[H-TRIP: Exact Many-to-Many Distance Index for Multi-Robot Exploration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08139v1)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Frontier-based exploration systems repeatedly evaluate geodesic travel costs between robot poses and candidate viewpoints, and tour-based planners consume the full pairwise distance matrix built from them. Recomputing these matrices with one graph search per source scales with the explored map size, whereas distance caches that refresh only locally affected or newly created frontiers can retain stale costs between surviving pairs when newly discovered free space creates a global shortcut. We present H-TRIP (Hierarchical Tropical Rank-one Incremental Propagation), an exact distance index grounded in Kleene algebra over the min-plus semiring for monotonically growing occupancy grids. H-TRIP partitions the known map into a spatial hierarchy with fixed boundary ports. Each unknown-to-free revelation is decomposed into efficient rank-one min-plus edge insertions at the affected leaf, and only decreased port-to-port summaries are propagated along its ancestor chain. A shared hierarchical lift evaluates the dense many-to-many matrix of shortest distances for all active query points on the occupancy map. We prove that sequential insertions preserve exact shortest-path distances on the grid. Benchmark experiments confirm exact agreement with reference searches on fully built maps and show that dense many-to-many queries at a few dozen viewpoints are up to two orders of magnitude faster than sequential k x BFS. On exploration runs with sixteen robots, in-loop queries on the growing occupancy graph are 2.21x-8.59x faster than co-scheduled k x BFS on maps with side 256, 512, and 1024 cells.</p></details> |  |
